# 12 Data Handling

## 12.1 Introduction and Key Concepts
In this chapter, you will learn how to:
* Develop a research question.
* Collect, classify, and organise data.
* Summarise, represent, and analyse data to answer your research question.

---

## 12.2 The Data Handling Cycle
The process of data handling is a continuous cycle. We represent it as a cycle because once we have analysed the results, we may discover new questions that require further research.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0001-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0001-00.png)

It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce learners to the geometric principles and algebraic formulas required to quantify the surface area and spatial capacity of various shapes.



### Definition: Research
**Research** is the act of studying a subject to uncover new facts.

---

## The Issue of Bias and Collecting Valid Data
When we want to find information, we must be careful not to influence the process to achieve a specific, predetermined result. This is essential for ensuring the validity of the data collected.

### 12.3 Developing Questions

#### The Aim of the Research
Before beginning the research process, it is essential to state the aim of the research clearly. The aim must be stated in a way that can be measured.

*   **Vague Aim:** "I want to find out what learners think about the school."
*   **Clear Aim:** "To find out learners' opinions about the condition of the school buildings and whether the facilities are adequate."



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 7" and the topic title "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It serves as an introductory signpost for a Mathematics or Technical Mathematics lesson, signaling the focus on geometric formulas and spatial calculations required by the CAPS curriculum.

### 12.4 Collecting Data

#### Validity
When collecting data, you must plan carefully to ensure the data is **valid**.

**DEFINITION: Valid**
In statistics, valid data means that the data represents the real world accurately.

#### Different Ways of Collecting Data
The aim of your research influences the method you choose to collect data. There are four primary methods:

1.  **Observation:** Watching and recording events or behaviors.
2.  **Interview:** Asking questions directly to individuals or groups.
3.  **Questionnaire:** Using a set of written questions to gather information from a group.
4.  **Database:** Using existing records or stored information.

# 12.4. Collecting Data

When conducting research, it is essential to choose a data collection method that is suitable for the specific type of research being performed. Below are four common methods of gathering data.

---

### 1. Observation
Observation involves collecting data without requiring personal contact. It is often used to record events or behaviors as they happen.

*   **Example:** Counting the number of vehicles crossing a specific intersection every hour.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, specifically identifying "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an organizational signpost to orient learners toward the core geometric concepts of quantifying two-dimensional surface area and three-dimensional space.



---

### 2. Interview
An interview is a method of data collection that usually takes place between two people: the **interviewer** and the **interviewee** (or respondent).

*   **Usage:** This method is chosen when it is convenient to speak to respondents directly.
*   **Example:** Determining whether customers are satisfied with the service provided by sales staff.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-02.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-02.png)


---

### 3. Questionnaire
A questionnaire is a predetermined set of questions provided to a number of respondents to complete.

*   **Advantages:** It is an effective instrument for gathering information from a large number of people, especially those spread over a wide geographical area where face-to-face contact is difficult.
*   **Best Practices:** 
    *   Always include a short explanation of the research purpose.
    *   Adhere to ethical and moral codes of conduct.
*   **Example:** The national population census of South Africa, which is conducted every ten years.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-03.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-03.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-03.png]**
> This text-based graphic serves as a heading for a mathematical application problem involving ratios and proportions. It introduces the practical context of a "paint conversion ratio," which requires learners to apply scaling principles to calculate quantities in real-world scenarios.



---

### 4. Databases
Sometimes, the required information is already stored in a database, meaning the researcher does not need to collect new data.

*   **Definition:** Databases are organised lists of data. They can be computerised, or kept in books or paper filing systems.
*   **Example:** The list of learners at your school.
*   **Advantage:** The data is already organised and is easy to access.

### Worked Example 1: Deciding on the best way to collect data

#### Question
Which method would be appropriate for collecting data for each of the cases below? Give a reason for your choice.

1. How many learners at your school know about tuberculosis (TB) and what their perceptions are.
2. Whether bank clients feel that they are treated professionally or not by the bank staff.
3. The symptoms of hospital patients with cancer.
   

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum resource, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce learners to the geometric principles and algebraic formulas required to determine the surface area and spatial capacity of various shapes.


4. The average age of all learners in Grade 10.

#### Solution

1. **Anonymous questionnaires** would be useful so that learners don't have to worry about answering incorrectly. **Interviews** by a skilled interviewer could be useful so that the interviewer could find out more about what the learners know and believe about TB.
2. **A questionnaire** that clients fill in while visiting a bank would be a convenient way to collect this information.
3. **Observation** (in the form of a medical examination) would be the best method.
4. This information could be most easily obtained from a **database**, e.g., from the school's register of learners, which should have all the learners' dates of birth.

***

### Data Collection Exercises

**Exercise 1: Identifying Data Types**
Determine the nature of the data for the following scenarios:
*   b) The number of hours each learner in your class slept last night.
*   c) The weight of all learners in your class.
*   d) Customers' opinions on the new design of a shop.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0005-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0005-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0005-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a mathematics textbook, featuring the labels "Chapter 7" and "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the core geometric concepts of quantifying two-dimensional space and three-dimensional capacity, which are essential for learners mastering spatial reasoning and measurement applications in the CAPS curriculum.



**Exercise 2: Developing Interview Questions**
Develop two or three interview questions you can use to gather information about:
*   a) Learners' opinions about how their school uses technology in the classroom.
*   b) Whether learners in your school have mobile phones.
*   c) The brands of cell phones that learners have.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0005-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0005-01.png)


---

### 12.4. Collecting Data: Deciding who to ask

**Key Concept: Population**
The group that we want to collect data from is called the **population**. In some cases, we can ask every person in the group we are interested in to answer a questionnaire.

### Data Handling: Populations and Samples

#### 1. Key Concepts
*   **Population:** The entire group that you are interested in studying (e.g., all learners at a school).
*   **Sample:** A small selection of a larger population or collection.

#### 2. Sample Bias
**Definition:** Sample bias occurs when a certain section of the population from which the sample is drawn is not representative of that population.

*   **The Goal:** As a researcher, you must ensure your sample represents the population well.
*   **Example:** If you want to research the attitudes of learners toward physical exercise, surveying only the soccer team would result in **sample bias**. The soccer team is more likely to enjoy exercise than the average learner, meaning they do not represent the entire school population.

#### 3. Random Sampling
**Definition:** A sample is random if every member of the population has the same chance of being selected.

*   **Methodology:** To avoid bias, you should use a method that gives every individual an equal opportunity to be chosen.
*   **Example:** Asking every tenth learner who arrives at the school gate in the morning is a method of random sampling.
*   **Important Note:** Even when using random sampling, you must ensure that the resulting group is still representative of the population.

#### 4. Visual Representations



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0006-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0006-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0006-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum resource, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce learners to the geometric principles and algebraic formulas required to quantify the surface area and spatial capacity of various shapes.


*Figure 12.1: An example of a population is all the learners at a school.*



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0006-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0006-01.png)

*Figure 12.2: An example of a sample is a small (representative!) group of learners from the same school.*

#### 5. Data Validity
*   **Respondent Count:** The higher the number of respondents, the more valid your data will be.
*   **Questionnaire Design:** Developing a good questionnaire is essential for ensuring the information collected is accurate and valid.

### 12.4. Collecting Data: Designing Questionnaires

When collecting data, it is essential to ensure that enough people complete your questionnaire. If the response rate is too low, the information gathered may not accurately reflect the real situation.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 7" and the topic title "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It serves as an introductory signpost for a Mathematics or Technical Mathematics lesson, signaling the focus on geometric formulas and spatial calculations required by the CAPS curriculum.



#### Tips for Creating Clear and Accurate Questionnaires

To ensure your questionnaire is effective and that people are willing to complete it, follow these guidelines:

1.  **Keep it short:** Do not include information that you already know or that is unnecessary.
2.  **Evaluate each question:** Write down all relevant questions, then analyze them by asking:
    *   **Necessity:** Is this question necessary? If not, remove it.
    *   **Feasibility:** Is it possible for the respondent to answer? (e.g., Do not ask for information from five years ago that they are unlikely to remember).
    *   **Honesty:** Will the respondent answer the question honestly?
    *   **Efficiency:** Can the question be answered quickly?

**Note on Sensitive Information:**
You can make questions easier to answer by using **categories** instead of asking for precise numbers. People are often uncomfortable sharing exact details like age, weight, or salary. Grouping these into ranges makes the questionnaire more user-friendly.

#### Types of Responses

When designing your questions, choose between these two formats:

*   **Open-ended questions:**
    *   **Definition:** The respondent answers in their own words.
    *   **Advantage:** Provides insightful data and avoids biased answers.
    *   **Disadvantage:** Respondents may skip these questions if they take too long to answer.
*   **Closed-ended questions:**
    *   **Definition:** The respondent chooses from provided options (e.g., ticking a box).
    *   **Advantage:** Very convenient and quick for the respondent to complete.

#### Final Checklist for Questionnaire Design

Before finalizing your questionnaire, perform these final checks:

1.  **Check the wording:** Ask yourself, "Will people be able to understand this question?"
2.  **Logical flow:** Decide on an order of questions that is easy for the respondent to follow and understand.

# Activity 12 – 2: Developing a Questionnaire

## Objective
Collect information on the topic: "The heights of learners in your class."



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an introductory signpost to orient learners toward the core geometric competencies required for calculating the spatial dimensions of 2D and 3D shapes.



## Data Collection Approaches
When gathering data, you may choose one of the following three methods:

1.  **Questionnaire:** Best used when there are many learners to interview, as it is an efficient way to gather data.
2.  **Observation:** Appropriate for gathering a rough estimate of data.
3.  **Using a database:** Heights can be obtained directly from existing school or clinic records.

---

## Questionnaire Example
To conduct a survey, use a structured format. Below is an example of a questionnaire for height:

*Hi there! We are conducting a survey to get information about the heights of learners in this school. Please tick the correct box below.*

| Height Category | Tick Box |
| :--- | :--- |
| Shorter than $140\text{ cm}$ | |
| $140 - 149\text{ cm}$ | |
| $150 - 159\text{ cm}$ | |
| $160 - 169\text{ cm}$ | |
| $170\text{ cm}$ or taller? | |

---

## Observation Sheet
When collecting measurement data through observation, use a tally table to record the frequency of each height range:

| Range of heights ($\text{cm}$) | Number of learners |
| :--- | :--- |
| Shorter than $140\text{ cm}$ | |
| $140\text{ cm} - 149\text{ cm}$ | |
| $150\text{ cm} - 159\text{ cm}$ | |
| $160\text{ cm} - 169\text{ cm}$ | |
| Taller than $170\text{ cm}$ | |

---

## Practice
For further practice, visit the Intelligent Practice Service using the code: **24QV**

# 12.5 Classifying and Organising Data

## Classifying Data

After data has been collected, the next stage in the data handling process is to classify it. There are two main ways data can be classified:

### 1. Categorical Data
This is data that cannot be measured numerically but can be described.
*   **Examples:** The gender of a person (male or female), or the colours of sweets in a jar.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the labels "Chapter 7" and "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts required for learners to apply formulas to determine the surface area and capacity of various two-dimensional and three-dimensional shapes.



### 2. Numerical Data
This is data that can be measured by using numbers.
*   **Examples:** The height of a person or the number of books in your bag.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png]**
> This diagram is a geometric representation of a rectangular prism divided into sections, featuring labels A, B, C, and D positioned within the structure. It illustrates the concept of spatial partitioning and volume calculation, helping learners visualize how a three-dimensional object can be subdivided into distinct regions for mathematical analysis.



#### Sub-classifications of Numerical Data
Numerical data is further classified into **discrete data** and **continuous data**:

*   **Discrete Data:** Data where each value can only have one specific value that can be counted.
    *   *Example:* The number of bicycles ($1, 5, 8$, not $\frac{1}{2}$ a bicycle) or the number of puppies in a litter ($3$ puppies, $6$ puppies, not $\frac{1}{3}$ of a puppy).
*   **Continuous Data:** Data that can have any value within a range.
    *   *Example:* A person's weight may be anything between $50\text{ kg}$ or $100\text{ kg}$, including $67,4\text{ kg}$ or $78,3\text{ kg}$. The amount of rainfall is also continuous data as it can be any amount and is not limited to specific whole numbers.

---

## Organising Data

Data can be organised by using:

*   **Tallies:** Tallies are a way of counting how many of each group there are. The advantage of using tallies is that you can keep a running total.

### Data Handling: Organizing Data

#### 1. Tally Tables
A tally table is used to record data as it is counted. Each item is represented by a single vertical mark. To make counting easier, marks are grouped into sets of five. When the fifth item is reached, a diagonal line is drawn through the previous four marks.

**Example: Car Colours**

| Colour | Tally |
| :--- | :--- |
| Red | $\cancel{||||} ||$ |
| Green | $|||$ |
| Blue | $||||$ |
| Yellow | $||$ |



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0010-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0010-00.png)


#### 2. Frequency Tables
A frequency table lists categories or groups of data alongside the number of times each item occurs (the frequency). The frequency corresponds to the total count from the tally table.

**Example: Car Colours Frequency**

| Colour | Frequency |
| :--- | :--- |
| Red | 7 |
| Green | 3 |
| Blue | 4 |
| Yellow | 2 |

#### 3. Class Intervals
When dealing with large sets of data (e.g., the heights of learners), it is more efficient to group the data into specific bands called **class intervals**.

**Example: Heights of 32 Grade 10 Learners**

| Height range | Number of learners (Frequency) |
| :--- | :--- |
| $< 1,20\text{ m}$ | 1 |
| $1,20\text{ m} - 1,29\text{ m}$ | 0 |
| $1,30\text{ m} - 1,39\text{ m}$ | 2 |
| $1,40\text{ m} - 1,49\text{ m}$ | 4 |
| $1,50\text{ m} - 1,59\text{ m}$ | 7 |
| $1,60\text{ m} - 1,69\text{ m}$ | 11 |
| $1,79\text{ m} - 1,79\text{ m}$ | 7 |
| $> 1,80\text{ m}$ | 0 |

> **NOTE:** When using intervals for continuous data (e.g., $1,50\text{ m} - 1,59\text{ m}$), the lower boundary is included in the interval, but the upper boundary is not. For example, a measurement of exactly $1,60\text{ m}$ belongs to the interval $1,60\text{ m} - 1,69\text{ m}$.

### Worked Example: Working with Grouped Data

#### Question
The following grouped frequency table represents the heights of young plants (seedlings). Note that in each interval, the lower boundary is included, but the upper boundary is excluded.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0011-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0011-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0011-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an introductory signpost to orient students toward the study of geometric formulas and spatial dimensions required for calculating the surface area and capacity of various shapes.



| Height of seedling (mm) | Frequency |
| :--- | :--- |
| 10 - 14 | 3 |
| 15 - 19 | 6 |
| 20 - 24 | 7 |
| 25 - 29 | 5 |
| 30 - 34 | 4 |

**Questions:**
1. How many plants were measured altogether?
2. How many plants are less than $20\text{ mm}$ high?
3. How many plants are more than $25\text{ mm}$ high?
4. Into which interval would you place a plant that is $29,8\text{ mm}$ high?
5. How many plants are at least $25\text{ mm}$ high?

#### Solution
1. Total plants: $3 + 6 + 7 + 5 + 4 = 25$ plants.
2. Plants less than $20\text{ mm}$: $3 + 6 = 9$ plants.
3. Plants more than $25\text{ mm}$: $5 + 4 = 9$ plants.
4. A plant of $29,8\text{ mm}$ falls into the interval $25 - 29\text{ mm}$.
5. Plants at least $25\text{ mm}$ high: $5 + 4 = 9$ plants.

***

### 12.6 Summarising Data

After data has been collected, classified, and organised, it is not always possible to mention every individual piece of data in a report. Instead, we summarise data by describing the whole data set using a few representative numbers. Summarising data makes it easier to analyse the data later.

---

# Measures of Central Tendency and Measures of Spread

Data can be summarised using two primary methods:
*   **Measures of central tendency:** A single value that represents the central position of a data set.
*   **Measures of spread:** A description of how the data is spread out or dispersed.

There are three main types of measures of central tendency: **mean**, **mode**, and **median**.

---

## The Mean
The mean (or average) is the most common measure of central tendency. It is calculated by finding the sum of all values and dividing by the total number of observations.

### Formula
$$\text{Mean} = \frac{\text{Sum of the observational values}}{\text{Number of observations}}$$

**Example:**
For the data set: $2; 6; 8; 10; 12; 14; 18$
$$\text{Mean} = \frac{2 + 6 + 8 + 10 + 12 + 14 + 18}{7} = \frac{70}{7} = 10$$

---

## Worked Example 3

### Question 1
Find the mean of the numbers: $4; 6; 7; 3; 4; 8; 4; 2; 9$

**Solution:**
1. Sum of values: $4 + 6 + 7 + 3 + 4 + 8 + 4 + 2 + 9 = 47$
2. Number of observations: $9$
3. Mean: $\frac{47}{9} \approx 5.22$

### Question 2
Calculate the mean mark from the frequency table below for 20 learners:

| Mark | 4 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 | 10 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Frequency** | 2 | 4 | 3 | 6 | 3 | 2 |

**Solution:**
To find the mean from a frequency table, multiply each mark by its frequency, sum those products, and divide by the total number of learners ($20$).

$$\text{Sum} = (4 \times 2) + (6 \times 4) + (7 \times 3) + (8 \times 6) + (9 \times 3) + (10 \times 2)$$
$$\text{Sum} = 8 + 24 + 21 + 48 + 27 + 20 = 148$$
$$\text{Mean} = \frac{148}{20} = 7.4$$

---

### Calculating the Mean

The **mean** (average) is calculated by finding the sum of all values in a data set and dividing that sum by the total number of values.

#### Worked Examples

**Example 1: Simple Mean**
To find the mean of the data set: $4; 6; 7; 3; 4; 8; 4; 2; 9$
1.  **Sum the values:** $4 + 6 + 7 + 3 + 4 + 8 + 4 + 2 + 9 = 47$
2.  **Count the number of values:** There are $9$ values.
3.  **Calculate:** $\text{Mean} = \frac{47}{9} = 5,2$

**Example 2: Mean using Multiplication (Frequency)**
To find the mean of marks for $20$ learners where some marks are repeated:
1.  **Calculate the total:** $(4 \times 2) + (6 \times 4) + (7 \times 3) + (8 \times 6) + (9 \times 3) + (10 \times 2) = 148$
2.  **Calculate:** $\text{Mean} = \frac{148}{20} = 7,4$

---

### Activity 12 – 3: Calculating the Mean

**1. Find the mean of each of the following data sets:**
*   a) $5; 7; 19; 24; 10; 17; 21; 6; 22; 5; 9$
*   b) $4; 3; 1; 6; 1; 3; 8; 2; 4; 3$
*   c) $24; 14; 41; 34; 26; 30; 25; 19; 27$
*   d) $190; 215; 187; 208; 212; 202$

**2. Soccer Team Heights**
The heights, in centimetres, of boys in the first soccer team are: $175; 168; 175; 176; 173; 168; 169; 176; 169; 191; 176$. Find the mean height of these boys.

---

### Statistics: Mean and Median

#### 1. Calculating the Mean from Frequency Tables
To find the mean of data presented in a frequency table, you multiply each value by its frequency, sum these products, and divide by the total number of observations.

**Exercise 5: Find the mean for the following data sets:**

a) Time taken to complete class work (minutes)
| Time (minutes) | 6 | 9 | 10 | 13 | 15 |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Frequency** | 4 | 4 | 5 | 4 | 3 |

b) Age of learners (in years)
| Age (years) | 14 | 15 | 16 | 17 | 18 |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Frequency** | 2 | 3 | 10 | 15 | 10 |

---

#### 2. The Median
The **median** is the middle value in a set of data when the values are arranged in order of size.

*   **Ascending order:** Arranged from smallest to largest (e.g., $2, 3, 4, 5, 6$).
*   **Descending order:** Arranged from largest to smallest (e.g., $6, 5, 4, 3, 2$).

When finding the median, we must consider two scenarios:
1.  When there is an **odd** number of data values.
2.  When there is an **even** number of data values.

---

#### Worked Example 4: Finding the median with an odd number of values

**Question:**
Find the median of the numbers: $4; 6; 7; 4; 3; 4; 8; 2; 9; 7; 2$.

**Solution:**
First, arrange the numbers in ascending order:
$2; 2; 3; 4; 4; 4; 6; 7; 7; 8; 9$

| 2 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 4 | **4** | 6 | 7 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| | | | | | $\uparrow$ | | | | | |

The middle value is $4$. Therefore, the median is $4$.

---

### Finding the Median (Even Number of Values)

When a data set has an even number of values, there is no single middle number. To find the median, you must calculate the average of the two middle values.

#### Worked Example 5
**Question:** Find the median of the numbers: $4; 6; 7; 2; 3; 8; 9; 7; 4$

**Solution:**
1. Arrange the numbers in ascending order:
   $2; 3; 4; 4; 4; 6; 7; 7; 8; 9$

2. Identify the middle position:


![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a mathematics textbook, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts required for learners to calculate the surface area and capacity of various 2D and 3D shapes as prescribed by the CAPS curriculum.


| 2 | 3 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 6 | 7 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | | | | | $\uparrow$ | | | | |

3. Since there is no single middle number, take the two values at the center ($4$ and $6$) and find the value halfway between them:
   $$\text{Median} = \frac{4 + 6}{2} = 5$$

---

### Mode

The **mode** is the data value that appears most often in a set of data. 
*   No calculation is required to determine the mode.
*   If no number is repeated, there is no mode for the list.
*   A data set can have more than one mode.
*   For **grouped data**, we use the **modal class**, which is the group or class with the highest frequency.

#### Worked Example 6
**Question:** Find the mode of the set of numbers: $4; 6; 7; 6; 3; 4; 8; 4; 2; 9$

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-01.png)

> This graphic displays four geometric figures labeled A, B, C, and D, representing three-dimensional rectangular prisms and a triangular prism with specific dimensions in metres. It serves as a visual exercise for learners to apply volume and surface area formulas to different spatial shapes, requiring them to distinguish between the properties of prisms based on their provided length, width, and height measurements.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-03.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-03.png)

---

### Data Handling: Mode and Range

#### 1. The Mode
The mode is the value that appears most frequently in a data set.

**Worked Example: Identifying the Modal Colour**
*   **Question:** Funeka records the colours of schoolbags arriving at school: 27 blue, 16 red, 43 white, 7 black, 16 green. What is the modal colour?
*   **Solution:**
    1.  Identify the highest frequency: The value 43 appears most often.
    2.  The modal colour is white.

---

#### 2. The Range
The range is a measure of spread. It indicates how spread out the data values are by calculating the difference between the largest and smallest values in the set.

**Formula:**
$$\text{Range} = \text{highest data value} - \text{lowest data value}$$

**Worked Example 7: Finding the Range**
*   **Question:** Find the range of the numbers: $3; 7; 8; 5; 4; 10$.
*   **Solution:**
    *   Identify the lowest value: $3$
    *   Identify the highest value: $10$
    *   Calculate the range: $10 - 3 = 7$



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts of quantifying two-dimensional space and three-dimensional capacity, which are essential for learners to master spatial reasoning and practical measurement applications.



**Note:** Always subtract the lowest value from the highest value. Do not reverse the order (e.g., $3 - 10$ is incorrect).

### Activity 12 – 4: Calculating the mean, mode, median and range

#### Key Concepts
*   **Mean:** The average of a data set, calculated as: $\text{Mean} = \frac{\text{Sum of all values}}{\text{Number of values}}$
*   **Mode:** The value that appears most frequently in a data set.
*   **Median:** The middle value of a data set when the numbers are arranged in ascending order. If there is an even number of values, the median is the average of the two middle values.
*   **Range:** The difference between the highest and lowest values in a data set: $\text{Range} = \text{Maximum} - \text{Minimum}$

---

#### Exercises

**1. Find the mean, mode, median and range for each of the following data sets:**
*   a) $5; 7; 19; 24; 10; 17; 21; 6; 22; 5; 9$
*   b) $190; 215; 187; 208; 212; 202$

**2. The heights, in centimetres, of the girls in the cross-country running team are:**
$175; 168; 175; 176; 173; 168; 169; 176; 169; 191; 176$
Find the mean, mode, median and range of the height of these girls.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png]**
> This graphic displays a table header for a data handling exercise, featuring the categorical variable "Gender" and the continuous numerical variables "Height (m)" and "Weight (kg)." It introduces the foundational CAPS concept of organizing raw data into structured formats, which is the essential first step for learners to perform statistical analysis and identify trends within a dataset.



**3. Find the mean, median, mode and range of each of the following data sets:**
*   a) $46; 32; 18; 6; 19; 32; 81; 24; 49; 33$
*   b) $124; 214; 341; 134; 126; 130; 325; 319; 227$

**4. Here is a list of the maximum temperatures for a week, in degrees Celsius:**
$16; 3; 15; 25; 20; 19; 19$



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-01.png)


*   a) Give the mean, median, mode and range of the temperatures.
*   b) If the person who read the temperatures discovered that they had made a mistake and the $3$ degrees was meant to be $23$ degrees, how would this affect your summary of the data?

# 12.7 Representing Data

After developing a research question and collecting data, the next steps are to classify, organise, and summarise the data using measures of central tendency and spread. Finally, the data must be represented and interpreted graphically.

Common types of graphical representations include:
*   Pie charts
*   Histograms
*   Single bar graphs
*   Line graphs
*   Broken line graphs

---

## Pie Charts

Pie charts are used to compare parts of a whole. They do not show changes over time. A pie chart is divided into sectors, usually labeled as percentages, where each sector represents a specific proportion of the data. While they allow for easy reading of proportions, they are not suitable for identifying trends over time.

### Activity 12 – 5: Understanding Pie Charts



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a mathematics textbook, featuring the text "Chapter 12" and the topic "Data Handling." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce the CAPS curriculum module focused on the collection, organization, analysis, and interpretation of statistical data.



**Data Analysis:**
The pie chart represents the "Grade 10 Maths Lit November 2012 Exam Results" categorized by levels of achievement.

| Level of Achievement | Percentage |
| :--- | :--- |
| Level 7 | 2% |
| Level 6 | 4% |
| Level 5 | 9% |
| Level 4 | 15% |
| Level 3 | 20% |
| Level 2 | 40% |
| Level 1 | 10% |

#### Questions:

1.  **Which level did most of the learners obtain?**
    *   Level 2 (represented by the largest sector).

2.  **What was the percentage of learners who obtained this level?**
    *   $40\%$

3.  **Few learners achieved level 7. What was the percentage of learners at this level?**
    *   $2\%$

4.  **If there were 120 learners who wrote the examination, how many learners achieved level 4?**
    *   To calculate the number of learners, multiply the total number of learners by the percentage for Level 4 ($15\%$ or $0,15$):
    *   $\text{Number of learners} = 120 \times 0,15$
    *   $\text{Number of learners} = 18$

---

### Bar Graphs (EMG78)

Bar graphs are used to display data that is compared in categories.

**Example:**
A store tracks the number of customers visiting over a five-month period.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0019-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0019-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0019-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and the topic "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a CAPS-aligned textbook to introduce the mathematical and statistical concepts required for collecting, organizing, and interpreting data sets.



---

### Histograms (EMG79)

Histograms differ from bar graphs in that they usually represent **continuous data**. The data displayed on a histogram is grouped into intervals.

**Example: Foot lengths of learners in a class**

| Foot length | Frequency |
| :--- | :--- |
| $22 - 23,9\text{ cm}$ | $3$ |
| $24 - 25,9\text{ cm}$ | $10$ |
| $26 - 27,9\text{ cm}$ | $8$ |
| $28\text{ cm and longer}$ | $4$ |

We can represent this information on a histogram.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0019-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0019-01.png)

# 12. Data Handling

## 12.2 The Data Handling Cycle

### Different ways of collecting data

#### Activity 12 – 1: Deciding on the best way to collect data

**1. Which method would you use to collect data for each of the following?**

| Scenario | Suggested Method |
| :--- | :--- |
| a) The number of pens each learner in your class has. | Questionnaire or observation |
| b) The number of hours each learner in your class slept last night. | Questionnaire |
| c) The weight of all learners in your class. | Questionnaire or database (e.g., Physical Education records) |
| d) Customers' opinions on the new design of a shop. | Interview |

**2. Develop two or three interview questions you can use to get information about:**

*   **a) Learners' opinions about how their school uses technology in the classroom.**
    *   *Solution:* Learner-dependent answer.
*   **b) Whether learners in your school have mobile phones.**
    *   *Solution:* Learner-dependent answer.
*   **c) The brands of cell phones that learners have.**
    *   *Solution:* Learner-dependent answer.

### Data Collection Approaches

When conducting a survey, you may choose one of the following three methods:

1.  **Questionnaire:** Best used when there are many learners to interview, as it is efficient.
2.  **Observation:** Appropriate for gathering a rough estimate of data.
3.  **Database:** Using existing records (e.g., school or clinic records) to obtain specific information like height.

#### Questionnaire Example: Height Survey
To collect data on learner heights, you can use a tick-box format:

| Height Range | Tick Box |
| :--- | :--- |
| Shorter than $140\text{ cm}$ | |
| $140 - 149\text{ cm}$ | |
| $150 - 159\text{ cm}$ | |
| $160 - 169\text{ cm}$ | |
| $170\text{ cm}$ or taller | |

#### Observation Sheet
Use this table to record measurements during an observation:

| Range of heights ($\text{cm}$) | Number of learners |
| :--- | :--- |
| Shorter than $140\text{ cm}$ | |
| $140\text{ cm} - 149\text{ cm}$ | |
| $150\text{ cm} - 159\text{ cm}$ | |
| $160\text{ cm} - 169\text{ cm}$ | |
| Taller than $170\text{ cm}$ | |

---

### 12.4 Classifying and Organising Data
**Measures of central tendency and measures of spread**

#### Activity 12 – 3: Calculating the Mean
The mean (average) is calculated by finding the sum of all values in a data set and dividing by the total number of values ($n$):
$$\text{Mean} = \frac{\sum \text{values}}{n}$$

**Exercises: Find the mean for the following data sets:**

a) $5; 7; 19; 24; 10; 17; 21; 6; 22; 5; 9$

b) $4; 3; 1; 6; 1; 3; 8; 2; 4; 3$

c) $24; 14; 41; 34; 26; 30; 25; 19; 27$

### Statistics: Calculating the Mean

The **mean** (arithmetic average) is calculated by finding the sum of all values in a data set and dividing by the total number of values ($n$).

$$\text{Mean} (\bar{x}) = \frac{\sum x}{n}$$

For grouped data (frequency tables), the mean is calculated by multiplying each value ($x$) by its frequency ($f$) and dividing by the total frequency ($\sum f$):

$$\text{Mean} (\bar{x}) = \frac{\sum (f \cdot x)}{\sum f}$$

---

### Worked Examples

#### 1. Calculating Mean from Raw Data
**Problem:** The heights (in cm) of boys in a soccer team are: $175; 168; 175; 176; 173; 168; 169; 176; 169; 191; 176$.
*   **Sum of heights:** $175 + 168 + 175 + 176 + 173 + 168 + 169 + 176 + 169 + 191 + 176 = 1916$
*   **Number of values ($n$):** $11$
*   **Calculation:** $\frac{1916}{11} \approx 174,18$
*   **Mean:** $174,18 \text{ cm}$

**Problem:** Marks of 14 learners: $4; 5; 6; 7; 8; 8; 6; 9; 9; 2; 10; 3; 5; 6$.
*   **Sum of marks:** $88$
*   **Number of values ($n$):** $14$
*   **Calculation:** $\frac{88}{14} \approx 6,29$
*   **Mean:** $6,29$

---

#### 2. Calculating Mean from Frequency Tables

**Problem:** Calculate the mean amount of pocket money per week.

| Pocket money (Rand) | 30 | 35 | 40 | 45 | 50 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Frequency ($f$)** | 5 | 5 | 10 | 8 | 2 |

*   **Calculation:** $\frac{(30 \times 5) + (35 \times 5) + (40 \times 10) + (45 \times 8) + (50 \times 2)}{5 + 5 + 10 + 8 + 2}$
*   **Sum:** $\frac{150 + 175 + 400 + 360 + 100}{30} = \frac{1185}{30} = 39,50$
*   **Mean:** $R 39,50$

---

### Exercises: Frequency Tables

**a) Time taken to complete class work**

| Time (minutes) | 6 | 9 | 10 | 13 | 15 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Frequency** | 4 | 4 | 5 | 4 | 3 |

*   **Mean:** $10,35 \text{ minutes}$

**b) Age of learners**

| Age (years) | 14 | 15 | 16 | 17 | 18 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Frequency** | 2 | 3 | 10 | 15 | 10 |

*   **Mean:** $16,7 \text{ years}$

# Activity 12 – 4: Calculating the Mean, Mode, Median, and Range

## Key Concepts
*   **Mean:** The average of a data set, calculated by finding the sum of all values divided by the number of values.
*   **Mode:** The value that appears most frequently in a data set. If no value repeats, there is "none".
*   **Median:** The middle value of a data set when the numbers are arranged in ascending order. If there is an even number of values, it is the average of the two middle values.
*   **Range:** The difference between the highest and lowest values in a data set.

---

## Exercises and Solutions

### 1. Data Set Analysis
**Task:** Find the mean, mode, median, and range for:
a) $5; 7; 19; 24; 10; 17; 21; 6; 22; 5; 9$
b) $190; 215; 187; 208; 212; 202$

**Solutions:**
*   **a)** Mean: $13,18$; Mode: $5$; Median: $10$; Range: $24 - 5 = 19$
*   **b)** Mean: $202,33$; Mode: none; Median: $\frac{202 + 208}{2} = 205$; Range: $215 - 187 = 28$

---

### 2. Application: Cross-Country Team Heights
**Task:** The heights (in cm) are: $175; 168; 175; 176; 173; 168; 169; 176; 169; 191; 176$. Find the mean, mode, median, and range.

**Solution:**
*   Mean: $174,18 \text{ cm}$
*   Mode: $176 \text{ cm}$
*   Median: $175 \text{ cm}$
*   Range: $215 - 187 = 23 \text{ cm}$

---

### 3. Data Set Analysis
**Task:** Find the mean, median, mode, and range for:
a) $46; 32; 18; 6; 19; 32; 81; 24; 49; 33$
b) $124; 214; 341; 134; 126; 130; 325; 319; 227$

**Solutions:**
*   **a)** Mean: $34,1$; Mode: $32$; Median: $32$; Range: $81 - 6 = 75$
*   **b)** Mean: $212,56$; Mode: none; Median: $214$; Range: $325 - 124 = 201$

---

### 4. Temperature Data Analysis
**Task:** Maximum temperatures for a week: $16; 3; 15; 25; 20; 19; 19$

**a) Calculate the mean, median, mode, and range.**
*   Mean: $16,7$
*   Mode: $19$
*   Median: $19$
*   Range: $25 - 3 = 22$

**b) Correction:** If the $3^\circ\text{C}$ was meant to be $23^\circ\text{C}$, how does this affect the summary?
*   **Solution:** It would change the mean and the range. The mode and median would remain $19$.

# 12.7 Representing Data: Pie Charts

## Activity 12 – 5: Understanding Pie Charts



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0025-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0025-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0025-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and the topic "Data Handling." It serves as an organizational signpost within a CAPS-aligned textbook to introduce the mathematical and statistical concepts required for collecting, organizing, and interpreting data.



The pie chart above illustrates the Grade 10 Mathematical Literacy November 2012 Exam Results, categorized by levels of achievement.

### Analysis and Solutions

**1. Which level did most of the learners obtain?**
*   **Solution:** Level 2 (represented by the largest sector of the pie chart).

**2. What was the percentage of learners who obtained this level?**
*   **Solution:** $40\%$ of learners obtained this level.

**3. Few learners achieved level 7. What was the percentage of learners at this level?**
*   **Solution:** $2\%$

**4. If there were 120 learners who wrote the examination, how many learners achieved level 4?**
*   **Calculation:** 
    *   From the chart, Level 4 represents $15\%$.
    *   Number of learners = $15\% \times 120$
    *   Number of learners = $\frac{15}{100} \times 120 = 18$
*   **Solution:** 18 learners achieved level 4.

**5. Write the ratio of learners who achieved level 3 to those at level 2 in its simplest form.**
*   **Calculation:**
    *   Level 3 = $20\%$
    *   Level 2 = $40\%$
    *   Ratio = $20 : 40$
    *   Divide both sides by 20: $\frac{20}{20} : \frac{40}{20} = 1 : 2$
*   **Solution:** $1 : 2$

# Activity 12 – 6: Representing Data

## 1. Pie Charts
A pie chart is a circular statistical graphic which is divided into slices to illustrate numerical proportion.

### Worked Example: T-shirt Colours
**Problem:** There are 300 learners at a school sports day. The frequency of T-shirt colours is recorded below:

| Colour of T-shirt | Red | Blue | Green | Yellow |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Frequency** | 75 | 93 | 78 | 54 |

**Solution:**
The data is represented in the pie chart below:

## 2. Histograms and Frequency Tables
When dealing with a large set of numerical data, it is often useful to group the data into intervals (also called classes) to create a frequency table and a histogram.

### Worked Example: Learner Weights
**Problem:** The following list gives the weights of learners in a class (in kg):
64; 83; 74; 77; 65; 55; 58; 61; 63; 98; 97; 53; 54; 102; 78; 82; 86; 95; 67; 72

**a) Frequency Table**
Grouping the data into 10 kg intervals:

| Interval (in kg) | Frequency |
| :--- | :---: |
| 50 - 59 | 4 |
| 60 - 69 | 5 |
| 70 - 79 | 4 |
| 80 - 89 | 3 |
| 90 - 99 | 3 |
| 100 - 109 | 1 |

**b) Histogram**
A histogram uses bars to represent the frequency of data within specific intervals. Unlike a bar graph, the bars in a histogram touch each other because the data is continuous.

### 3. Data Representation: Line Graphs

**Problem Statement:**
The following table provides the maximum temperature (in $^\circ\text{C}$) for each month of the year.

| Month | Jan | Feb | Mar | Apr | May | Jun | Jul | Aug | Sep | Oct | Nov | Dec |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Max Temp ($^\circ\text{C}$)** | 27 | 28 | 25 | 21 | 20 | 17 | 18 | 19 | 20 | 24 | 25 | 27 |

**a) Draw a line graph of this data.**



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a navigational signpost to introduce the mathematical statistics and data analysis module within the CAPS curriculum, signaling the transition to a unit focused on collecting, organizing, and interpreting numerical information.



**b) Describe the trends you see in the data.**
*   **Solution:** The maximum temperature drops towards the middle of the year (June/July), which corresponds to the winter season.

### 4. Data Representation: Pie Charts

**Problem Statement:**
The pie chart below represents the favourite fruit juice flavours of a group of 120 learners.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-01.png)


**a) Calculate how many learners chose each type of juice.**

To calculate the number of learners for each category, multiply the total number of learners (120) by the percentage (expressed as a decimal):

*   **Fruit cocktail ($45\%$):** $120 \times 0,45 = 54$ learners
*   **Litchi ($30\%$):** $120 \times 0,30 = 36$ learners
*   **Grape ($12,5\%$):** $120 \times 0,125 = 15$ learners
*   **Apple ($12,5\%$):** $120 \times 0,125 = 15$ learners

**b) In what way does the pie chart work better than a bar graph to represent this data?**
A pie chart is more effective at showing the relationship of each part to the whole (the total group of 120 learners). It allows for an immediate visual comparison of the proportions (percentages) of the total population.

**c) What information would a bar graph give you that this pie chart does not?**
A bar graph would make it easier to compare the exact numerical differences between the categories. While a pie chart shows proportions, a bar graph allows for a more precise visual comparison of the actual quantities (the number of learners) for each flavour.

### Worked Example: Calculating Proportions from Percentages

**Problem:** Given a total of 120 learners, calculate the number of learners per category based on the following percentages:
*   Fruit cocktail: $45\%$
*   Litchi: $30\%$
*   Grape: $12,5\%$
*   Apple juice: $12,5\%$

**Solution:**
*   Fruit cocktail: $\frac{45}{100} \times 120 = 54$ learners
*   Litchi: $\frac{30}{100} \times 120 = 36$ learners
*   Grape: $\frac{12,5}{100} \times 120 = 15$ learners
*   Apple juice: $\frac{12,5}{100} \times 120 = 15$ learners

**Note on Pie Charts:** A pie chart is a simple, visual representation that works well for representing percentages. It allows us to see at a glance the relative proportions of the learners who prefer each flavour.

---

### Exercise: Interpreting Bar Graphs

**5. Look at the bar graph below and answer the questions that follow.**



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a navigational signpost to introduce the mathematical statistics and data analysis section of the CAPS curriculum.



**Questions:**
a) Does this graph tell us how many Grade 10 learners there are in total?
b) Can we assume that none of the learners who take Accounting take Geography?
c) A pie graph of this data would not make sense. Explain why.

**Solutions:**
a) **No.** It may look like there are 140 learners in total, but we have no way of knowing if that is correct or just an arbitrary number. Also, learners take more than one subject, so we cannot use the numbers of learners per subject to determine how many learners there are altogether.
b) **No**, we cannot assume this.
c) **Learners do not only take one subject.** Therefore, the data cannot be split into discrete percentages per subject and represented using a pie chart (as the total would exceed $100\%$).

# 12.8 Analysing Data

## Activity 12 – 7: The whole data handling cycle

1. **Design a data collection tool** for recording the favourite sport of each of your classmates.
   * **Solution:** Learner-dependent answer.

2. **Record, organise, summarise and represent data** on the favourite sport of each of your classmates.
   * **Solution:** Learner-dependent answer.

3. **Analyse the data** to determine which sports are the most popular and which are the least popular.
   * **Solution:** Learner-dependent answer.

# 12.9 End of chapter activity

## Activity 12 – 8: End of chapter activity

1. In your school, collect (anonymous) data about learners' performance in Grade 10 Mathematical Literacy during the June 2012 examination. This must be grouped in terms of the levels that each learner has achieved, e.g., Level 1 (0% - 29%), Level 2 (30% - 39%), and so on.

   a) Organise the data collected into a frequency table.
   b) Draw a histogram for the data collected, clearly label both axes and provide a meaningful heading.
   c) Referring to your histogram, clearly state which level most learners achieved, and what the reason for this trend could be.

   * **Solution:**
     a) Learner-dependent answer.
     b) Learner-dependent answer.
     c) Learner-dependent answer.

2. After the first term Mathematical Literacy test was written at Lerato Secondary School, the Head of Department sampled the scripts of 11 learners out of a class of 42. The results of these 11 learners were as follows (out of a total of 50 marks):
   $22; 16; 45; 35; 40; 25; 42; 37; 41; 35; 27$

### Data Handling: Statistical Analysis and Frequency Distributions

#### Worked Example: Analyzing a Set of Marks
Given a set of marks, we perform the following statistical operations:

**a) Arrange in ascending order:**
$16; 22; 25; 27; 35; 37; 40; 41; 42; 45$

**b) Mean:**
The mean is the average of the data set.
$\text{Mean} = 33,18$

**c) Median:**
The median is the middle value of the ordered data set.
$\text{Median} = 35$

**d) Mode:**
The mode is the value that appears most frequently.
$\text{Mode} = 35$

**e) Range:**
The range is the difference between the maximum and minimum values.
$\text{Range} = 45 - 16 = 29$

**f) Convert mean to a percentage:**
To convert the mean to a percentage (based on a total of 50):
$$\frac{33,18}{50} \times 100 = 66,36\% \approx 66,4\%$$

---

#### Frequency Distribution: Citrus Tree Heights
Gaab, a citrus farmer, measured the height of 50 trees. The data is organized into the following frequency table:

| Height in cm | Number of trees |
| :--- | :--- |
| 0 - 139 | 3 |
| 140 - 149 | 7 |
| 150 - 159 | 20 |
| 160 - 169 | 12 |
| 170 - 179 | 5 |
| 180 - 189 | 2 |
| 190 - 199 | 1 |
| **Total** | **50** |

**Exercises:**

**a) Histogram Construction:**
Use the table above to draw a histogram. Ensure the graph includes:
*   A descriptive heading.
*   Labeled axes (x-axis: Height in cm; y-axis: Number of trees).

Identify the interval with the highest frequency (the modal class).

**c) Least common height interval:**
Identify the interval with the lowest frequency.
*   The least common height interval is $190 - 199\text{ cm}$.

### Data Handling: Rainfall Analysis

#### Exercise: Rainfall in Bethlehem (January 2012)
The following table records the rainfall in millimeters (mm) for the town of Bethlehem over a 7-day period:

| Day | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Rainfall (mm)** | 15 | 25 | 10 | 40 | 20 | 20 | 45 |

---

#### Worked Solutions

**a) Determine the mean rainfall**
The mean is the sum of all values divided by the number of observations ($n=7$).
$$\text{Mean} = \frac{15 + 25 + 10 + 40 + 20 + 20 + 45}{7} = \frac{175}{7} = 25 \text{ mm}$$

**b) Calculate the mode**
The mode is the value that appears most frequently in the data set.
*   The value $20$ appears twice. All other values appear once.
*   **Mode** = $20 \text{ mm}$

**c) Determine the median rainfall**
The median is the middle value when the data is arranged in ascending order:
$10, 15, 20, \mathbf{20}, 25, 40, 45$
*   The middle value (4th position) is $20$.
*   **Median** = $20 \text{ mm}$

**d) Calculate the range of the data**
The range is the difference between the maximum and minimum values.
$$\text{Range} = \text{Maximum} - \text{Minimum}$$
$$\text{Range} = 45 - 10 = 35 \text{ mm}$$
*(Note: The original text contains a calculation error in the range; the correct range is $35 \text{ mm}$.)*

**e) Line Graph**

### Data Handling: Pie Charts and Tables

#### Analysis of Movie Genres (Pie Chart)



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient learners to the specific curriculum section focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of data sets.



**Questions:**

a) What kind of movie is watched the most?
b) What percentage of all the movies watched is this (the most watched movie)?
c) According to the pie chart, some kinds of movies are watched the same amount. Which two pairs of movies are watched the same amount?
d) Suppose a sample of 200 people were interviewed:
    i. What is the number of people in the sample who watch action movies?
    ii. What is the number of people in the sample who watch science fiction movies?
e) Write the number of people who watch action movies and the number of people who watch science fiction movies as a ratio in its simplest form.
f) Give two reasons for why you think the percentages of people watching science fiction movies and foreign movies are the lowest.

**Solutions:**

a) Comedy movies.
b) $27\%$
c) Romance and Drama movies ($14\%$ each), and Foreign and Science Fiction movies ($8\%$ each).
d) 
    i. $18\%$ of $200 = \frac{18}{100} \times 200 = 36$ people.
    ii. $8\%$ of $200 = \frac{8}{100} \times 200 = 16$ people.
e) Ratio of Action to Science Fiction: $36 : 16$. 
   Dividing both sides by the highest common factor ($4$):
   $36 \div 4 = 9$
   $16 \div 4 = 4$
   Ratio = $9 : 4$
f) There may be fewer science fiction and foreign movies made or screened, or they may simply be less popular.

#### Melissa's Heart Rate Data

Melissa measures her heart rate every half hour during the morning. The results are recorded in the table below:

| Time of day | Heart rate |
| :--- | :--- |
| 9:00 a.m. | 65 |
| 9:30 a.m. | 69 |
| 10:00 a.m. | 72 |
| 10:30 a.m. | 110 |
| 11:00 a.m. | 90 |
| 11:30 a.m. | 72 |
| 12:00 p.m. | 75 |
| 12:30 p.m. | 75 |
| 1:00 p.m. | 69 |

### Data Handling: Interpreting Line Graphs

#### Exercise
a) Plot a line graph of the data in the table.
b) Describe what you notice about the graph.
c) Why do you think that her heart rate increases suddenly during the day?

---

#### Solution

**a) Line Graph Representation**



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0034-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0034-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0034-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the specific curriculum section focused on the collection, organization, analysis, and interpretation of statistical data.



**b) Observations**
Melissa’s heart rate increases suddenly at 10:30 a.m. Apart from this spike, it is fairly consistent, remaining within a small range.

**c) Analysis**
It is likely that her heart rate increased due to physical activity, such as going for a walk or a run.

> This scatter plot displays Olympic Men’s Marathon winning times against the year of the event, featuring a line of best fit to indicate the trend. The axes represent time (in hours and minutes) and the year, with data points plotted as individual diamonds. The graph conceptually demonstrates a negative correlation, illustrating how athletic performance has improved over time as winning times have steadily decreased.
